A Type Theory with a Tiny Object
Mitchell Riley

Abstract
We present an extension of Martin-Löf Type Theory that contains a tiny object; a type for which there is a right adjoint to the formation of function types as well as the expected left adjoint. We demonstrate the practicality of this type theory by proving various properties related to tininess internally and suggest a few potential applications.



1
Introduction

Tiny objects are central to Lawvere’s account of differential forms in
synthetic differential geometry
[
Law80
]
; a tiny object
\mathbb{T}
in a
category
\mathcal{C}
is one with the amazing property that the internal hom
functor
{(\mathbb{T}\to-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}}
has a right adjoint

{\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}-:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}}
. In synthetic differential geometry, the

infinitesimal interval
defined by

{D:\equiv\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid x^{2}=0\}}
is one such object. This
object corepresents tangent spaces so that
D\to X
is the tangent
space of
X
, and elements of
(D\to X)\to\mathbb{R}
are therefore
(not-necessarily linear) 1-forms on
X
. By tininess, such a form
corresponds to an element of
X\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}\mathbb{R}
, that is, 1-forms are
simply functions on
X
valued in a highly non-standard space.
Additional properties like linearity, closedness, etc. may be imposed
by carving out an appropriate subobject of
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}\mathbb{R}
. The notion
of tininess was anticipated by
[
KR79
,
Law79
]
but its utility was first made
explicit in
[
Law80
, §3]
, and it appears in much of
the ensuing work on synthetic differential
geometry
[
Law97
,
Law98
,
Law02
,
Law04
,
Law11
,
KR98
,
KR99
,
Koc06
]
.

Tininess is simultaneously unusual and abundant. Unusual because, in
the category of sets, only the singletons are tiny. This is easy to
check:
(\mathbb{T}\to-)
can only preserve coproducts when
\mathbb{T}\cong 1
.
And abundant because, for any category
\mathcal{C}
with finite products, the
representable presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
are all tiny. An explicit formula for
the right adjoint to
(\mathbb{T}\to-)
with
\mathbb{T}
representable is

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}Y(c):\equiv\mathsf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})(\mathbb{T}\to\mathsf{y}c,Y)
, as may be
verified by a little (co)end calculus. In favourable cases
representable sheaves are also tiny
[
MR91
, Appendix
4]
, but this is less common.

In this paper we tackle a challenge set by Lawvere, to produce a
formal system for working with tiny objects: “This possibility does
not seem to have been contemplated by combinatory logic; the formalism
should be extended to enable treatment of so basic a
situation”
[
Law04
, Section 3]
. We describe an
extension of Martin-Löf Type Theory that makes a fixed type
\mathbb{T}

tiny by introducing a type former
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
for the amazing right
adjoint to
(\mathbb{T}\to-)
. The situation is a little subtle. Freyd
proves that for any tiny object
\mathbb{T}
in a topos, the pullback of

\mathbb{T}
to any slice is also tiny
[
Yet87
, Theorem 1.4]
. But
the right adjoint witnessing this tininess is
not
stable under
base-change. We therefore have to add to the context structure of type
theory in order to support this operation.

We add
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
as a ‘Fitch style’
modality
[
Clo18
,
BCMEPS20
]
, where the type former

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
is made right adjoint to an operation on contexts. Such
modalities are particularly nice when they are a
double

right adjoint, and
FitchTT

[
GCKGB22
]
is designed for adding
modalities of this kind to MLTT. We are in such a situation, of
course, because

{(-\times\mathbb{T})\dashv(\mathbb{T}\to-)\dashv\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}-},

and so
we could use
[
GCKGB22
]
directly to produce a type theory.

But there is a special feature of
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
which impels us to create
a specialised theory for it. Specifically, the leftmost adjoint

(-\times\mathbb{T})
already exists as an operation on contexts: it is
simply context extension with
i:\mathbb{T}
. We allow
\mathbb{T}
to be an

ordinary type
, rather than a pre-type or special piece of
syntax. This flexibility is essential for applications in synthetic
differential geometry where many important tiny objects may be
constructed internally as ordinary types. The only new context former
needed is something corresponding to
(\mathbb{T}\to-)
, which we write
as context extension with a
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{}}
.

To make
(-,i:\mathbb{T})\dashv(-,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}})
on contexts, we need unit

\Gamma\to(\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}})
and counit

(\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}},i:\mathbb{T})\to\Gamma
substitutions. In
[
GCKGB22
]

these are added axiomatically, and the type theory is presented in a
variable-free CwF style where these explicit substitutions are pushed
around manually. The downside of the approach is that figuring out how
to use a variable could be potentially challenging: one may have to
devise by hand an explicit substitution that extracts the variable
from the context. One of our aims is to give a fully explicit variable
rule which builds in the normal forms of these ‘stuck substitutions’.

The reason this gets interesting is that, because
\mathbb{T}
is an
ordinary type, we can substitute
any
term
t:\mathbb{T}
for
i

in the counit substitution. And so, the admissible counit rule is
parameterised by a genuine term
t:\mathbb{T}
. The counit rule commutes
with all type and term formers and becomes stuck on the ordinary
variable rule, so every use of a variable in this theory will have
(possibly many) attached terms of
\mathbb{T}
corresponding to these stuck
counits.

Related Work.

In
[
LOPS18
]
, a tiny interval
\mathbb{I}
is used to construct,
internal to a 1-topos, a universe that classifies fibrations. This is
performed in ‘crisp type theory’, a fragment of Shulman’s spatial type
theory
[
Shu18
]
. The
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
type former is
described by a collection of axioms, and the fact that the adjunction
is external is enforced by requiring the inputs to these axioms to be
‘crisp’, roughly, protected by a use of the global sections/discrete
inclusion modality
\flat
. Internally, this manifests as an
equivalence

{\flat(A\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B)\simeq\flat((\mathbb{T}\to A)\to B)}
. An
alternative axiomatisation that is coherent for higher types is given
in
[
Mye22
, Appendix A]
, by instead asserting for each
crisp type
B
a counit map
(\mathbb{T}\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B)\to B
such that
the induced map

{\flat(A\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B)\to\flat((\mathbb{T}\to A)\to B)}
is an
equivalence.

These equivalences guarded by
\flat
are more restrictive than
necessary: we will prove an equivalence

{(A\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B)\simeq\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}((\mathbb{T}\to A)\to B)}
, where
A

and
B
do not have to be ‘global types’ (but the dependency of
B
is
still somewhat restricted).

The most comparable type theory to ours is presented
in
[
ND21
]
. The authors give rules for a right adjoint to
the
dependent product

\Pi_{\mathbb{T}}:\mathcal{E}/\mathbb{T}\to\mathcal{E}
rather than the
non-dependent function type, an operation which they call

transpension
. It is a theorem of Freyd
[
Yet87
, Proposition
1.2]
that this is equivalent to the apparently weaker
notion of tininess. Because we target the non-dependent function type,
our new judgemental structures and rules are much simpler than those
for transpension, and let us maintain admissibility of substitution
and (conjecturally) normalisation; for transpension it is unclear
whether these are achievable.

The context extension with the tiny type is also treated specially,
similar to accounts of internal
parametricity
[
CH20
,
Cav21
]

[
GCKGB22
, Section 5]
where the

\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T}
context extension is a special piece of syntax,
and so only special ‘terms’ may be substituted for it.

A benefit of these previous systems is that the precise notion of
tininess is easier to tweak. For example, whenever
\mathcal{C}
is monoidal,
representable presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
are
monoidally tiny
in that

(\mathbb{T}\multimap-)
has a right adjoint, where
\multimap
denotes the Day
internal hom. This non-cartesian tininess is central to forthcoming
work on Higher Observational Type
Theory
[
SAK22
]
, whose intended semantics are in
a non-cartesian cube category.

Contributions.

•

In
Section

2
, we introduce the new context structure
required to support the type former and in
Section

3
the
type-former itself.

•

In
Section

4
, we give a couple of useful
constructions that are definable internally.
Section

4.1

shows that we can derive induction principles for functions out of

\mathbb{T}
, and
Section

4.2
recovers the transpension
operation from our non-dependent right adjoint, providing a new
construction that applies in the univalent setting.

•

In
Section

5
, we discuss some potential
applications of the type theory and a prototype type-checker
utilising an extension of the usual normalisation by evaluation
algorithm.

Terminology.

There is a constellation of terminology
around tininess and similar notions, with “amazingly tiny”,
“atomic”, “infinitesimal”, “satisfying the ATOM property”,
“internally/externally projective” and “small-projective” all used
to refer to one of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T},-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
or

(\mathbb{T}\to-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
preserving epimorphisms, finite colimits,
colimits or having a right adjoint. With additional assumptions many
of these notions coincide, but we follow
[
Yet87
]
in
settling on simply “tiny” as the name for objects whose internal hom
has a right adjoint. The other properties play no role in this work.
There are also various notations for the right adjoint, including

(-)_{\mathbb{T}}

[
Yet87
]
and

(-)^{1/\mathbb{T}}

[
Law97
]
, and even

\nabla_{\mathbb{T}}

[
Yet87
]
and
{\between}-
for
transpension
[
ND21
]
. We follow
[
LOPS18
]
in using

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}-
.

Acknowledgements.

Thank you to David Jaz Myers, Andreas Nuyts and Jon Sterling for
helpful comments and suggestions. The author is grateful for the
support of Tamkeen under the NYU Abu Dhabi Research Institute grant
CG008.

2
Contexts and Variables

We take as our starting point the bare judgements of Martin-Löf Type
Theory. When using our theory we will also assume dependent sums,
products, intensional identity types and an infinite hierarchy of
universes, but the extension of type theory with a tiny object does
not require the presence of any other type-formers.

The
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
type-former will be added by making it right adjoint to
a judgemental version of “
\mathbb{T}\to\Gamma
” which we write as

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Here, to refer to it later, the lock is
annotated with a ‘lock name’
\mathcal{L}
. This first section will be
spent adding the necessary rules for the context
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}

to behave like functions into
\Gamma
.

A simple way to achieve this would be to assert axiomatic unit and
counit substitutions for the
(-\times\mathbb{T})\dashv(\mathbb{T}\to-)

adjunction as in the following:

\frac{\Gamma\,\,\mathsf{ctx}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{unit}_{\Gamma,i,\mathcal{L}}:\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}}\quad\textsc{left=unit-sub}\frac{\Gamma\,\,\mathsf{ctx}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}\vdash\mathsf{counit}_{\Gamma,\mathcal{L},i}:\Gamma}\quad\textsc{left=counit-sub}

together with equations that explain how these explicit substitutions
are pushed around and annihilated.

We can do better, however, and give normal forms for the placement of
these explicit substitutions. The examples in later sections show that
it is quite feasible to work in the resulting type theory by hand.

Besides the context lock, we only need one additional base rule: a
modified version of the variable rule that builds in stuck instances
of the counit substitution. The rule is completely structural and
independent of the rules for types (besides the existence of
\mathbb{T}
).
There are two new admissible rules, corresponding to precomposition
with the counit or unit substitutions.

We now describe the additions to MLTT one rule at a time.

The Tiny Type.

There is a closed type
\mathbb{T}
.

\frac{~}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{T}\,\,\mathsf{type}}\quad\textsc{left=tiny-form}

In applications, this will typically be an already-existing closed
type rather than a new one asserted axiomatically.

Context Locks.

There is a special context extension,

\frac{\Gamma\,\,\mathsf{ctx}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}\,\,\mathsf{ctx}}\quad\textsc{left=ctx-lock}

to be thought of as
\mathbb{T}\to\Gamma
. We call
\mathcal{L}
a ‘lock
name’, all lock names in a context are unique.

The Counit.

Because a locked context represents functions
into what comes before the lock, we can use variables to the left of
a lock if we can provide an argument to the function: this
corresponds to precomposition with the counit substitution, with a
substitution for
\mathbb{T}
and some contractions built-in.

The simplest situation we can encounter is

\frac{\Gamma\vdash a:A\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\mathbb{T}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash a[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:A[t/{\mathcal{L}}]}\quad\textsc{left=counit?,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}

corresponding (non-dependently) to the composite

\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}\to\Gamma)\times\Gamma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[\mathsf{id},t]}(\mathbb{T}\to\Gamma)\times\Gamma^{\prime}\times\mathbb{T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{pr}}(\mathbb{T}\to\Gamma)\times\mathbb{T}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}A

The new piece of term syntax is a stuck instance of this

[t/{\mathcal{L}}]
rule which we build into the variable rule below.
To distinguish the admissible rule from the stuck rule, we will
write the admissible rule as
[t/{\mathcal{L}}]
and the actual stuck
syntax as
\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket
. Roughly, the admissible
[t/{\mathcal{L}}]

will add a
\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket
to every
free
variable usage in

a
, much like the underlining operation in the type theory for

\natural

[
RFL21
]
.

We need to generalise this rule in a couple of ways. First, we need
to allow an additional telescope to be carried along, so that we may
go under binders in the term
a:A
.

Additionally, we may need to apply the counit to multiple locks
simultaneously. To facilitate this, for a context

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\,\,\mathsf{ctx}
let
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})
denote the (ordered)
list of context locks

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}
that appear
in the telescope
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

The generalised rule is then:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\,\,\mathsf{tele}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t_{i}:\mathbb{T}\text{ for }\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[t_{1}/{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,t_{n}/{\mathcal{L}_{n}}]\,\,\mathsf{tele}}\quad\textsc{left=counit-tele,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}\frac{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash a:A\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t_{i}:\mathbb{T}\text{ for }\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[t_{1}/{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,t_{n}/{\mathcal{L}_{n}}]\vdash a[t_{1}/{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,t_{n}/{\mathcal{L}_{n}}]:A[t_{1}/{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,t_{n}/{\mathcal{L}_{n}}]}\quad\textsc{left=counit,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}

Because we will so often be working with a sequence of terms and
locks, we will abbreviate

[t_{1}/{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,t_{n}/{\mathcal{L}_{n}}]
to simply

[\vec{t}/{\mathcal{L}}]
.

Formally, the telescope
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
should be annotated in the syntax
of the operation, as it is not inferable from the raw syntax.

Variable Usage.

To use a variable
x:A
that is
behind some context locks, we must provide a term
t_{i}:\mathbb{T}

for each lock between that variable and the front of the context.

\frac{\Gamma,x:A,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\vec{t}:\mathbb{T}\text{ for }\mathcal{L}\in\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})}{\Gamma,x:A,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x\llbracket\vec{t}/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:A[\vec{t}/{\mathcal{L}}]}\quad\textsc{left=var}

The type of the variable usage has the admissible counit rule
applied to it: typically these counits will not be stuck on the type
unless
A
is itself a variable.

Substitution.

Ordinary substitution into a variable
continues into the keys associated with the variable. When we find
the variable we are substituting for, the ‘stuck’ counits are turned
back into admissible ones, which then proceed into the substituted
term:

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[a/x]

\displaystyle\vdash(y\llbracket\vec{t}/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)[a/x]:\equiv y\llbracket\vec{t}[a/x]/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[a/x]

\displaystyle\vdash(x\llbracket\vec{t}/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)[a/x]:\equiv a[\vec{t}[a/x]/{\mathcal{L}}]

Computing the Counit.

We will now explain how the
counit operation is actually computed on syntax. We begin with the
simplest possible instance: the addition a single additional key with using a
closed term of
\mathbb{T}
:

\frac{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash b:B\cdot\vdash t:\mathbb{T}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]\vdash b[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:B[t/{\mathcal{L}}]}\quad\textsc{left=counit,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}

This is calculated by induction on
b
until we reach an instance of
the variable rule, say for a variable
x:A
.

As in the definition of single-variable substitution, there are
cases depending on where in the context
x
lies. The typical case
is when
x:A
is in
\Gamma
, so the variable usage looks like

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1},x:A,\Gamma_{2},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket:A[\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}]

where
\mathcal{J}
are the locks in
\Gamma_{2}

and
\mathcal{K}
are the locks in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. The new
[t/{\mathcal{L}}]
is slot into place, and left stuck:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1},x:A,\Gamma_{2},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]\vdash

\displaystyle x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},t/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

When working informally, this means counting how many

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}
are created between the application of

[t/{\mathcal{L}}]
and the variable usage (by the use of some of the
typing rules to be introduced later), and placing
t/{\mathcal{L}}

to the left of all these freshly created
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}_{i}}
.

If instead
x:A
is in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, the variable usage looks like

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime},x:A,\Gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket:A[\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}]

where
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{n}
are the locks in

\Gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. Access to
x
is not affected by the addition of the
lock
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
(which is placed to the left of it), so the
variable usage is left unchanged:

\displaystyle\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}[t/{\mathcal{L}}],x:A[t/{\mathcal{L}}],\Gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x\llbracket\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

The counit is more complex to evaluate when arbitrary open terms of

\mathbb{T}
are involved. Two complications can arise:

•

First,
t:\mathbb{T}
may not be closed, so that in

\Gamma_{1},x:A,\Gamma_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\mathbb{T}
, the
variables used in the term
t
are now locked behind the fresh locks
in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
by the time we reach the variable
x
. We therefore
apply
[\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}]
to the term
t
, so:

\displaystyle x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},t[\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}]/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

•

Instead, the terms
\vec{j}
and
\vec{k}
may not be closed,
and so some of the the variables used in them might now lie behind
the new lock
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
. The term
t
is used to unlock them:

\displaystyle x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x\llbracket\vec{j}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{J}},t/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

•

Finally, and slightly horrifyingly, the two complications can happen
at the same time, yielding the final, general definition:

\displaystyle x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[t/{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x\llbracket\vec{j}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{J}},t[\vec{k}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}}]/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}[t/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

Examples of the Counit.

The fully general rule is the
worst-case scenario; the vast majority of cases encountered in
practice are simpler. First, the counit commutes with ordinary
term-formers such as pairing:

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N},y:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(x,y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N},y:\mathbb{N},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(x,y)[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(x\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket,y\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

Any variables which are bound in a term do not get annotated with the new counit:

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(\lambda y.x+y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(\lambda y.x+y)[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(\lambda y.x\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket+y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

When the variable
y:\mathbb{N}
is bound, it is added to the context

after
the lock
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the variable usage therefore
needs no annotation to be well-formed. In particular, the rule has no
effect on closed terms:

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\vdash 2

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash 2[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv 2

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}

The counit is also applied to the type of a term, and is computed in a
similar way.

\displaystyle A:\mathcal{U},B:A\to\mathcal{U},f:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}B(x)

\displaystyle\vdash f

\displaystyle:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}B(x)

\displaystyle A:\mathcal{U},B:A\to\mathcal{U},f:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}B(x),\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash f[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle:\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}B(x)\right)[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv f\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A[i/{\mathcal{L}}])}}}{\prod_{(x:A[i/{\mathcal{L}}])}}{\prod_{(x:A[i/{\mathcal{L}}])}}{\prod_{(x:A[i/{\mathcal{L}}])}}(B(x))[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv f\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)}}}{\prod_{(x:A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)}}{\prod_{(x:A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)}}{\prod_{(x:A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)}}B\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket(x)

Because
x
is bound in the
\Pi
type-former, its usage is not
annotated in the codomain
B\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket(x)
. The type-family

B\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket
has type

(A\to\mathcal{U})\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket\equiv(A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket\to\mathcal{U})
, so
applying it to
x\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket
is well-formed.

When a variable already has a stuck counit attached, the result of
applying an additional counit will depend on the relative position of
the two locks. For the simplest possible case, the variable usage

x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket:A,

there are
already a few possibilities.

•

If the new lock is placed at the end of the context, the terms
used on the existing lock will also end up with the stuck counit on
their variables.

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket k[i/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}},i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket k\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{K}},i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

•

Moving one step to the left, we may place the new lock in
between
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
k:\mathbb{T}
. In this case neither of the
variables
i
or
k
are locked, and so do not need stuck counit of
their own to be used.

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket k[i/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}},i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}},i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

•

One step further, we may place the new lock
before
an
existing one, in which case the term
i
is the one which gains a
stuck counit in order to be used:

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket i[k[i/{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}}]/{\mathcal{L}},k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket i[k/{\mathcal{K}}]/{\mathcal{L}},k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{L}},k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

•

Finally, we may place the new lock at the start of the context,
in which case the variable usage is not changed at all:

\displaystyle x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},i:\mathbb{T},x:A,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[i/{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:A

Unit.

To complete the implementation of the adjunction,
we have an admissible rule representing the unit map. The following
rule is precomposition with the substitution

\Gamma\to(\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}})
, suitably generalised
with a telescope
\Gamma^{\prime}
:

\frac{\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}\,\,\mathsf{tele}}{\Gamma\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]\,\,\mathsf{tele}}\quad\textsc{left=unit-tele,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}\frac{\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash a:A}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]\vdash a[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]:A[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]}\quad\textsc{left=unit,fraction={-{\,-\,}-}}

As with the
[t/{\mathcal{L}}]
operation, we use substitution-like
syntax for the unit map because, like substitution, it commutes past
everything until it reaches a variable. The
\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.
notation is
chosen to match the syntax that is used for the type-former later.

The lock and key then ‘click together’, yielding an ordinary
substitution. In the simplest case, when
t:\mathbb{T}
is closed and

x
has a single matching stuck
counit
.

\displaystyle x\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x[t/i]

If
t
is an open term, the unit rule must continue into
t
:

\displaystyle x\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]:\equiv x[t[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]/i]

Of course, because
x
is already a variable there are only two
possibilities: either it is equal to the variable
i
bound in the
unit rule or it is not.

\displaystyle i\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle:\equiv t[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle x\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle:\equiv x\qquad\text{otherwise}

If
x
has several stuck counits, the operation needs to continue
into the other stuck counits.

\displaystyle i\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},t/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle:\equiv{}t[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle x\llbracket\vec{j}/{\mathcal{J}},t/{\mathcal{L}},\vec{k}/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle:\equiv{}x\llbracket\vec{j}[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{J}},\vec{k}[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket

In contrast to the counit, this rule is never stuck. After any
application of
[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
, the lock
\mathcal{L}
and the
variable
i
no longer appear in the resulting term

a[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
.

Examples of the Unit.

Now, applying the unit rule. Similar to the counit, the unit commutes
with ordinary type formers (in these cases having no effect, because
the variables used come after the lock).

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},x:\mathbb{N},y:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(x,y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N},y:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(x,y)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(x[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}],y[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}])

\displaystyle\equiv(x,y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},x:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(\lambda y.x+y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\vdash(\lambda y.x+y)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(\lambda y.x[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]+y)

\displaystyle\equiv(\lambda y.x+y)

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

If the variable used is not the one that the unit is being applied to
then it remains unchanged:

\displaystyle x:A,i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:A

\displaystyle x:A,k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(x\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv x[k/i]

\displaystyle\equiv x:A

The simplest interesting case is a variable usage that matches the
variable that the unit is being applied to.

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv i[k[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]/i]

\displaystyle\equiv k[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv k:\mathbb{T}

That is not the only way we could have gained access to
i
. We can
use the term of
\mathbb{T}
just constructed to gain access to
i
a
second time:

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash i\llbracket i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash\left(i\llbracket i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket\right)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv k[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv k:\mathbb{T}

Nothing stops us from iterating this forever:

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash i\llbracket i\llbracket i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:\mathbb{T}

Semantically, the context corresponds to
i:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
and

k:\mathbb{T}
, and the term is an incarnation of the iterated
application
i(i(\dots i(k)))
. The unit
[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
replaces
i

with the identity, and so the entire term reduces to
k
regardless of
how many iterations we do.

The result of applying the counit may be different for each variable,
depending on what terms are used in the attached counit:

\displaystyle i:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},j:\mathbb{T},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(i\llbracket j/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket,i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket):\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle j:\mathbb{T},k:\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\vdash(i\llbracket j/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket,i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(i\llbracket j/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}],i\llbracket k/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}])

\displaystyle\equiv(i[j/i],i[k/i])

\displaystyle\equiv(j,k):\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}

At this point it may appear as though the counits are just delayed
substitutions that are ‘activated’ by the unit, but this is not the
case. Suppose we have global elements
1,2,3,4:\mathbb{T}
, and
consider a term

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{T},y:\mathbb{T},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\vdash(x\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket,y\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket):\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}

Applying two different substitutions allows us to select which of the
stuck keys to apply:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\equiv{}}(x\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket,y\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket)[i/x][j/y][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(i\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket,j\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(i\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}],j\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}])

\displaystyle\equiv(1,4)

and

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\equiv{}}(x\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket,y\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket)[j/x][i/y][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(j\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket,i\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket)[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}]

\displaystyle\equiv(j\llbracket 1/{\mathcal{L}},2/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}],i\llbracket 3/{\mathcal{L}},4/{\mathcal{K}}\rrbracket[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}][\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}j./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}])

\displaystyle\equiv(2,3)

We will be able to cause these different substitutions to occur once
we have the type-former. So the
user
of a term containing stuck
keys gets to choose which of the terms is eventually used, but doesn’t
get to choose the actual term that gets plugged in. And the stored
terms for the other locks are completely lost!

These rules are peculiar, but if we insist on pushing the admissible
rules to the leaves it seems something like this is forced on us by
the setting we are trying to capture.

3
The Amazing Right Adjoint

The type-former is made right adjoint to
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
in the
‘obvious’ way, following the pattern of
[
Clo18
,
BCMEPS20
,
GCKGB22
]
.

\frac{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}\vdash A:\mathcal{U}}{\Gamma\vdash\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A:\mathcal{U}}\quad\textsc{left=$\surd$-form}\frac{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}\vdash a:A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.a:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A}\quad\textsc{left=$\surd$-intro}\frac{\Gamma,i:\mathbb{T}\vdash r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A}{\Gamma\vdash r(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.):A[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]}\quad\textsc{left=$\surd$-elim}\\
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.a)(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)\equiv a[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]r\equiv\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.(r[i/{\mathcal{L}}](\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.))

In words:

•

\surd
-form
and
\surd
-intro
: For any
term
a:A
, that uses all ambient variables locked behind

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we can bind the lock
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
and form a term

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.a:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A
. The term syntax is intended to be
reminiscent of an ordinary
\lambda
-abstraction.

Reading upwards, going under
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}
or
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.-
means
that all extant variables become locked by
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
, so every
future use of those variables in
a
must have an attached

-\llbracket t/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket
to be well formed.

These are precisely the formation and introduction rule for a
dependent right adjoint type
[
BCMEPS20
]
.

If
A
is a closed type, we will just write
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
rather than
binding an unused lock name.

•

\surd
-elim
: If using an additional assumption

i:\mathbb{T}
we can produce a term
r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A
, then we can
amazingly apply
r
to the fresh
i
to form

r(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.):A[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
. We are free to ignore the new
assumption
i
in the term
r
if we wish, just as a constant
function may ignore its argument.

The syntax is, put mildly, unorthodox: to keep the analogy with
ordinary function application, the variable
i
is bound in the
parentheses on the right, but then is
in scope in the body of
the ‘function’ to the left
1
1

1

If we took Lawvere
seriously
[
Law06
]
and wrote
function application as
xf
, then we would not have this
problem!
.

•

\surd
-beta
: Binding a lock
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.a
and
amazingly applying the result to
\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.
reduces to

a[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
, with

(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.a)(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)\equiv a[\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i./\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}]
in perfect
analogy with
(\lambda x.b)(a)\equiv b[a/x]
.

•

\surd
-eta
: Any term
r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A
is equal to
the term that binds a lock and immediately amazingly applies
r
to
it, in analogy with
f\equiv(\lambda x.f(x))
.

3.1
Basic Examples

To begin, some maps that are easily definable from the rules. The
functor
\mathbb{T}\to-
is a monad, and its right adjoint
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
is
therefore a comonad.

Definition 3.1
.

If
A
is (for now) a closed type, define an
extract map
\mathsf{e}_{A}:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A\to A
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}_{A}

\displaystyle:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A\to A

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}_{A}(r)

\displaystyle:\equiv r(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)

In words, to get a term of
A
we need a term of
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
that
we can amazingly evaluate on an additional assumption
i:\mathbb{T}
.
But we already have the variable
r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
, so we have no
need to use the additional assumption.

We have no way of quantifying over
closed
types internally, so
any definition mentioning closed types should be considered
provisional: a corresponding fully internal definition will be given
shortly after.

Definition 3.2
.

If
f:A\to B
is a (for now) closed function, define
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f
by

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f

\displaystyle:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B

\displaystyle(\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f)(r)

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.f(r\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.))

This should be compared with the definition of function
post-composition, as the shape of the definition is the same:

\displaystyle f\circ-

\displaystyle:(C\to A)\to(C\to B)

\displaystyle(f\circ-)(r)

\displaystyle:\equiv\lambda c.f(r(c))

In words, we start with
r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
. To produce a term of

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B
we have to produce term of
B
, but with all our present
assumptions locked behind
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
. There is a function

f:A\to B
available, so we just need access to a term of
A
. We
cannot amazingly evaluate
r:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
to get this term of
A
,
because
r
has become trapped behind
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
. But we
can

amazingly evaluate
r\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A
on an additional
assumption
i:\mathbb{T}
. This gets us an argument of type
A
that we
can apply
f
to. This time, the presence of the additional assumption

i:\mathbb{T}
is crucial: it is exactly what we needed to gain access
to
r
.

If
A
is not a closed type, then the type of

\mathsf{e}_{A}:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A\to A
is not well-formed: once we go
under
\surd
, we lose access to the variable
A
. Similarly, if

f
is not a closed function, then we cannot produce

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}A\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}B
, even if the types
A
and
B

are themselves closed, because the function
f
(as a variable) also
becomes locked behind
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
when the argument
r
does.

However: if
all
the inputs to the above constructions are
provided under
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}
, then we can have access to them where we
need it. Suppose instead that
A:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}\mathcal{U}
. Then, we may use

A
as an input to
\surd
by forming

\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.):\mathcal{U}
. Our map
\mathsf{e}_{A}

can now be correctly typed:

Definition 3.3
.

For
A:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}\mathcal{U}
, define a map
\mathsf{e}_{A}

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}_{A}:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)\to A(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}_{A}(r):\equiv r(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)

And similarly for functoriality:

Definition 3.4
.

For
A,B:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}\mathcal{U}
and

f:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}\left((\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}i.A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)\to(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}i.B\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)\right)
, define
\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f
by

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f

\displaystyle:\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}\left(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}i.A\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket\right)\to\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd_{\hskip-2.04861pt\mathcal{L}}$}}\left(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}i.B\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\raisebox{0.70007pt}{$\SavedStyle\surd$}}f(r)

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}j.f\llbracket j/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}i.r\llbracket i/{\mathcal{L}}\rrbracket)

This simple example is already very noisy, so to cut down on symbols
we introduce two pieces of syntactic sugar.

First:

Like ordinary
\to
-intro
, and as with any
negative type-former, the
\surd
-intro
rule is
right-invertible and so we will define terms of it via copattern
matching. For example, we will write

\displaystyle r(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}):\equiv\mathcal{J}\quad\text{to mean}\quad r:\equiv\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{K}}.\mathcal{J}

This integrates nicely with ordinary copattern-style definitions of
functions: we write

\displaystyle f(x,y,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}},z):\equiv\mathcal{J}\quad\text{to mean}\quad f:\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.\lambda z.\mathcal{J}

This syntax makes it easy to see at a glance which locks affect each
variable in the term
\mathcal{J}
: the
x
and
y
here are to the left of

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
in the list of arguments and so are locked by

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}
, whereas
z
is not locked at all.

Similarly, we might evaluate some combination of
\to
and
\surd

by applying it to a tuple of arguments, as in
f(a,b,\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.,c)
.
The ‘amazing’ argument to a lock is a new bound variable
i
as in

\surd
-elim
, but remember that this variable is now in
scope to the left of that argument in the tuple
and
in the
function itself. This form of evaluation computes in the ‘obvious’ way,
with a
\beta
-reduction for each entry in the list of arguments. If

f
is defined via the pattern match above, we find

\displaystyle f(a(i),b(i),\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.,c)

\displaystyle\equiv f(a(i))(b(i))(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)(c)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\lambda x.\lambda y.\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.\lambda z.\mathcal{J}\right)(a(i))(b(i))(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)(c)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.8}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}{\scalebox{0.4}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}}_{\mathcal{L}}.\lambda z.\mathcal{J}[a(i)/x][b(i)/y]\right)(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{185.0}{$\lambda$}}}}{}i.)(c)
